My aunt, who in her mild imagination was Ginger Rogers lacking an Astaire, lived to the last a notch above her station, an exiled queen propped up by Medicare. A mirrored sideboard’s porcelain swans and swains (no Meissen, some Limoges, all very late) amount now to the bulk of her remains: even en masse, the daintiest estate. Bring bubble wrap and drive them to the thrift store. It’s not our place to ponder the true worth of fragile imitations, fortune’s gifts for ladies of noble taste and common birth. This poem appears in the October 2026 print edition.
The Atlantic
This Is What Women’s Self-Defense Looks Like
2026-09-13

This Is What Women’s Self-Defense Looks Like

The Atlantic | 2026-09-13

Visit Original Website
This article was featured in the One Story to Read Today newsletter. Sign up for it here. On the first day of self-defense class, I learn to say “no.” We’re in a Southern California martial-arts studio not far from an In-N-Out Burger. The space smells faintly of cooked meat. I’m one of six students, all women: four nurses, one flight attendant, and me. In this introductory session, we’re supposed to get a taste of what it means to protect ourselves—and, if necessary, to fight back. For the longest time, I had wondered what it would be like to hit someone. Like, really hit them. I had grown up understanding that women aren’t supposed to engage in fantasies about violence, which is to say that we aren’t really supposed to think about it. But many of us do. All the time. Violence can, of course, affect anyone. It is in our homes and neighborhoods and in the news; it is experienced person to person, nation to nation, directly and indirectly. But for women especially, even when violence is not an immediate physical reality, what is real is the constant threat of it. In my experience, this sense of threat doesn’t change as time goes on. So in late 2023, I signed up for self-defense classes with a training program called Impact Personal Safety. I was working on a book exploring the ways in which women and girls are socialized not to say “no,” even to people who threaten them. But I also just wanted to understand what it is for “no” to be expressed in the body—what it means to feel the “no.” Our lead instructor, Dawn Hofmeister, is in her 50s and petite. She stands straight and appears to glide through the air, and although I’m much larger than she is, I feel submissive in her presence. She teaches alongside a couple of female assistants and a male sparring partner, Matt Harris. A middle-aged man with doe-like eyes, Harris tells the assembled group that he will be playing the role of our assailant that day, a character named Billy. Everyone likes Harris. Nobody likes Billy. Billy emerges after Harris slips on a padded suit and helmet. In the scenarios we’re thrown into, Billy is aggressive. He walks right at us, his voice raised. He can also be sneaky: Sometimes, he’ll sidle up in a bumbling, faux-friendly way before lunging. Other times, he appears to be severely mentally ill, or under the influence of drugs, or perfectly sober but intensely pushy. He asks for things (the time, a cellphone) or whether we need something—help with our groceries? A ride? When we rebuff him, he starts toward us with purpose. “No!” we yell. This is supposed to come after some effort at placating him—I don’t need a ride, thank you; I think the person you’re looking for went the other way—a tactic that, in these scenarios, does not work, in that it does not thwart Billy from approaching, bull-like, with apparent malicious intent. Hofmeister explains that she teaches students to say “no” repeatedly and often because it helps strengthen a woman’s resolve to assert her boundaries and puts a potential assailant on notice that what he’s about to do is undesired. She adds that the word can increase the power of a physical blow and disrupt a freeze response, keeping the woman breathing through her counterattack. Along with saying “no,” we’re taught the palm-heel strike—heels of the hands up and together, palms facing outward, ready to hit—the first line of physical defense in a sequence of moves and postures meant to end with Billy on his back or side, writhing. The second move is a strong knee to the groin. The third, once he’s down, is a knee or stomp to the head. Billy takes a fair amount of abuse. Sometimes I worry about Harris, the human being inside the padded suit, especially when he is slow to get back on his feet. But feeling bad for Billy, I realize, is part of what I’m trying to undo: the internalized empathy response that can make people, especially women, afraid to hit another person, or lead them to give bad actors the benefit of the doubt even while the voice in their own head is saying danger. When Billy advances toward us, my first “no” is the weakest. But then my hands go up and I yell “No!” with more force. Billy falls, and I jab my heel into his chin. My knee goes to his groin—another “No!” Soon the no’s are coming quickly and easily, and by the time Billy is splayed on the ground, I’m hovering over him—like Muhammad Ali standing over Sonny Liston in that famous 1965 photo—and practically screaming. Contemporary self-defense courses recognize that protecting oneself involves a lot more than elbowing an assailant in the neck. As Susan Brownmiller put it in her 1975 book, Against Our Will: Men, Women and Rape, telling women to kick a man in the groin or stick a thumb in his eye is not enough—because first, women must overcome an aversion to hitting. Describing her time learning self-defense, she wrote that this inhibition “was so strong in each of us that on the first try none of us could make physical contact.” What women need, she argued, “is systemic training in self-defense that begins in childhood, so that the inhibition resulting from the prohibition may be overcome.” What many people think of as women’s self-defense tends to go by the designation Empowerment Self-Defense, or ESD. Also known as “feminist self-defense” and “holistic self-defense,” ESD posits that teaching nonphysical strategies is as important as teaching physical ones—because if a woman can recognize and thwart an attacker’s verbal intrusions early in an encounter, she is less likely to need to get physical. Impact Personal Safety, Southern California, the program I trained under, says that it has taught assertiveness, boundary-setting, deflection, and de-escalation to tens of thousands of students—plus the skills necessary for what the organization’s late founder, Lisa Gaeta, described as “causing a scene.” (Essentially: raising your voice and shouting things such as “Get away from me” and “I don’t want this,” which is harder to do than you might think.) That makes ESD different from mainstream self-defense, which “tends to be stranger-focused, sometimes fear-based, rather than empowerment-based,” Jocelyn Hollander, a University of Oregon sociology professor and self-defense instructor, told me. ESD’s emphasis on “internal” skills—emotional, psychological—is aimed at subverting behaviors that students might have adopted based on their gender or personal history, or in response to political and cultural messaging. Basically, Hollander said, the method gives women the tools they need to say no “to whatever someone wants to do to or with you.” ESD is applicable not only in extreme interactions but also in what some might call the more routine situations that many women experience, such as being coerced into sex. In Hollander’s classes, she has students turn to each other with a simple question—“Can I borrow your pencil?” or “Do you want to go have coffee?”—to which the other person then has to respond “No.” Usually, there’s a lot of nervous laughter, Hollander told me. Many students realize that they never say “no” to anybody. Or, when they do, they qualify it, soften it, make excuses. But once they discover that the world won’t end when they say “no,” Hollander observed, it can be a “huge epiphany.” Such training, Hollander’s research suggests, pays tangible dividends. Women who take ESD classes are less likely to be the victim of rape or sexual assault than women who do not. In a 2020 study of female college students involving nearly 300 women, Hollander found that those who had taken an ESD class had about 50 percent lower odds, on average, of experiencing any sort of assault over the one-year period following the course than those who had not taken a class. “I just have to point out that 50 percent is a huge decrease for an intervention,” she said. “Psychologists are usually happy if they get a few percentage points’ difference. Fifty percent is immense.” Her research has also found that many students experience increased “self-efficacy”—a sense that they can be effective in the world. And other research has found that this sort of self-defense training can reduce feelings of anxiety, depression, fear, and self-blame. Hollander’s results have been echoed in the work of other academics, including Charlene Senn, a University of Windsor psychology professor whose research focuses on male violence against women. Senn was inspired by the academics Patricia Rozee and Mary Koss, who in 2001 published a paper titled “Rape: A Century of Resistance,” which synthesized decades of research on sexual violence. They translated their analysis into a series of recommendations, such as giving women cognitive and emotional education to defend themselves against assault. Senn took those recommendations and set out to make them come alive. First, she created a program for university women called Enhanced Assess, Acknowledge, Act—a 12-hour, evidence-based training that, among other things, teaches women how to recognize a risk of sexual assault and then resist it. Then, from 2011 to 2013, she and a group of academics conducted a study of 893 female college freshmen in Canada, to test the program’s efficacy. Of the students, 451 were assigned to a “resistance group” and took EAAA classes, and 442 were put in a control group, which meant that they basically “went to a room,” Senn said, and read sexual-assault-prevention pamphlets distributed by their schools. After the EAAA courses, Senn said, the resistance and control groups were given follow-up surveys at intervals of one week, six months, 12 months, and 18 months. The researchers found that one year after the EAAA training, the women in the resistance group were 46.3 percent less likely to have been raped and 63.2 percent less likely to have experienced attempted rape than the women in the control group. A later study by Senn and her colleagues, which looked at a broader, more diverse cohort of university women who took EAAA classes from 2017 to 2021, had similar findings: a 57.3 percent reduction in reports of rape and a 32.9 percent reduction in attempted rape six months after the training. To me, these results were nothing short of stunning. I wondered why data like these hadn’t been publicized more widely. Jill Cermele, a psychology professor at Drew University, told me that this was partly because of ideology. Cermele co-authored a 2022 paper with Martha McCaughey, an academic specializing in women’s studies and the author of Real Knockouts: The Physical Feminism of Women’s Self-Defense, about the ways in which the efficacy of self-defense has been neglected or ignored by both public-health officials and anti-rape activists—some of whom contend that emphasizing the need for women to learn self-defense serves only to put the onus on them, rather than on men. Cermele said she believes that this logic is misguided—that no matter people’s views on blame, anyone who cares about women’s safety has “a professional and a moral obligation to pay attention to the data.” That said, “one of the things that psychology tells us is that people are not convinced by data—they’re convinced by stories.” During my next set of self-defense classes, things take a darker turn. The 16-hour “basics” training unfolds over the course of two weekends, with a new cohort of students. Our lead instructor is Dani, a young woman who took her first Impact class when she was only 6. Our padded assailant is Michael Peñafiel, a middle-aged father of a young son. Like Harris, our attacker from the intro class, Peñafiel has an alter ego: Kane. On Day 1, we hit, we kick, we send knees into Kane’s groin. Yet the stakes feel somehow lower. Partly, it’s the absurdity of Kane’s outfit—a suit of hard plastic, foam, and duct tape, with a harness-like thing slung between his legs and around his waist. His helmet makes him look like a bug. The padding forces hims to move around the studio with his hips thrust forward. But then comes Day 2: what we glibly call “sexual-assault day.” Our instructors teach us how to fend off an assailant who approaches while we’re lying on our side, and demands that we get into positions that could—quickly, disturbingly, unmistakably—leave us open to assault. We get down on hands and knees. We lie flat on our backs. Today, our attacker is closer, the interaction more intimate. This makes everything scarier and can provoke a freeze response that feels impossible to overcome. Read: Everything we know about rape is wrong Kane gets behind me, clutches my arms, and pins me to the floor. He crouches on his knees, breathing heavily over me. I think: Maybe if I just lie here, he will go away. I’m surprised at how heavy he is and how difficult it is to imagine freeing myself from his grasp. He hisses instructions. “What’s going on?” I ask him. We’re supposed to say this phrase out loud, not so much to prompt a reasonable answer but to remind ourselves that we are being attacked in the first place. He merely mumbles. Dani tells me to roll onto my back, which is the last thing I want to do. “Be patient,” she says. “He’s going to make a mistake.” And make a mistake he does. As Kane tries to position himself directly on top of me, he releases one of my arms and up it goes, my fingers in a beak-like shape, directed at his eyes. “Eyes! Eyes! Eyes!” everyone yells. I stab at the netting on his padded helmet, and Kane falls backward. Then I get into a kick position, leg in the air and ready to strike—his head, his torso, his groin, wherever I can find a target. After a few kicks, Kane finally goes down, at which point I pull myself up and run around to the top of his head to assess that he’s indeed knocked out. Finally, I run away, keeping in mind the final step: go get help. I should acknowledge that self-defense techniques may not help in situations when it is impossible or dangerous for a woman to fight back—such as when she is incapacitated, or when an assailant is armed and threatening deadly force. And it is fair to argue that the potential perpetrators of violence, not their victims, should be the ones subject to intervention and training. But Impact’s founder, Gaeta, who died last year, had no patience for the argument that to avoid victim blaming, women should not be taught to defend themselves. People say, “Well, we should make men be better,” she once told me. “How the fuck am I going to make men be better?” The point about responsibility is not a new argument. In the ’80s and ’90s, for instance, some feminist activists began to express concern that teaching women self-defense was akin to engaging in victim blaming—so the practice became “unfashionable in a way,” Hollander told me. She added that yes, ideally, more men would move through life with the explicit understanding that they should not assault women, “but from a researcher’s perspective, we have no clue how to make that happen.” No intervention has been shown to meaningfully reduce perpetration. “And what are we supposed to do in the meantime?” Hollander said. “Just sit around and wait for the revolution?” Some researchers argue that when it comes to men, self-defense training for women can move the needle in an important way—by shifting men’s own understanding of their place in the world. “Changing men does not necessarily entail intervening directly with men,” Hollander wrote in a 2018 article. Quoting a paper by the masculinity researcher Michael Flood, she added, “Empowering women may transform gender relations by ‘shifting the wider conditions within which men make choices about violence and non-violence.’” The third day of basics class is the day I start crying. We’re learning how to react defensively and offensively: Many of the techniques involve moving toward our assailant, not away from him. This is counterintuitive—instinctively, one wants to retreat from danger—but being able to inflict real damage and disable an attacker means having to get up close. The disabling doesn’t go well for me. About three hours into the class, I’m thrown to the mat and pinned. Kane is on top of me and feels stronger and heavier than usual, more hell-bent on restraining me. Just when I think I’ve started to get away, he grabs me again and pins me on my side, my back to him, my arms folded in front of me. I freeze. The instructors and other students yell words of encouragement—“He’s going to make a mistake!” someone calls—but I can’t move. The words are meaningless. Adrenalin is making me tremble. After what seems like an eternity, Kane seems to ease up a bit, and I’m able to turn toward him, hit him, and stumble away. I make my way to Gaeta—present that day, watching from a table at one end of the studio—who asks, “What happened?” The question is part of the drill. After the fight, we’re supposed to describe in as much detail as possible what occurred and what our attacker looked like, to practice how, in the real world, we might give specific information about an assault to a friend or law-enforcement officer. I’m not sure what to tell her, but I try: “He attacked me. He threw me to the ground. I tried to get up and almost succeeded, but he pinned me back down.” I feel shame and embarrassment rising, and I begin to cry. I run to the line of students assembled against a wall. One says, “Good job.” The others don’t know what to say. Neither do I. I just stand there with tears streaming, my heart thumping. Two weeks after my graduation from the class, I’m stuck on that memory, of an encounter that felt like failure. Peñafiel has since clarified that he did not, in fact, ease up on me. He also said that my reaction—freezing, crying—is not uncommon. Driving home from that class, shaken, I had called my friend Nancy, a documentary filmmaker whose work has focused largely on sexual assault. She tried to put what had happened to me into perspective: I got scared, she said, and felt shame and embarrassment, which came not only from being scared but also from my sense that I hadn’t fought back in the “right” way. About a week after the class, I also spoke with one of Impact’s assistant instructors, Amanda Hall, who is also a reality-TV producer. She told me that she has seen this sort of response many times. She herself cried during the opening drill in her first class. The adrenaline can be “really activating,” she said. “It’s scary no matter what.” I try to give myself a little grace. After all, I was able to finish the fight. This reminds me of something that Cermele, the psychology professor, told me: Many people mistakenly believe that once an assault happens or starts, you have to somehow wait until the assailant is finished. This is not always the case. “Unfortunately, assaults take time,” she said, which means “multiple opportunities for resistance. And if I couldn’t organize myself to resist in the beginning, if I was too drunk, if I was too afraid, if I was whatever, there might still be a moment where I can do something with that. And I think we miss that by not talking about resistance. It’s very all-or-nothing.” She told me about a student who, after learning self-defense techniques one semester, was assaulted by a male friend’s roommate at a college party. During the attack, the woman was able to summon her skills and fight him off. “We would call that a success story,” Cermele said. “The assault ended because of what she did, not because he decided it was over.” I now know what it feels like to hit someone. I also can’t stop thinking about how different the world might be if more girls and women were taught to defend themselves. We might see the number of assaults against women cut in half. We might see more teenagers like a self-defense student who told me that now she knows how to use her voice: “I feel like I’m a powerful person.” We would see more women learning to shout “No!” and enjoying, with much less fear, what one instructor called “a bigger life”—the kind of life every one of us deserves. ​​When you buy a book using a link on this page, we receive a commission. It’s All Fun and Games Until You Give AI Your Credit Card
2026-09-13

It’s All Fun and Games Until You Give AI Your Credit Card

The Atlantic | 2026-09-13

Visit Original Website
In the race to develop intelligent machines, the leading AI companies have built bots that can whip up spreadsheets at a moment’s notice, write sophisticated code, and even solve long-standing math problems. But when it comes to the more mundane tasks of life—such as ordering groceries or booking flights—these tools sometimes still struggle. Now a new bot called Instinct is outperforming the AI giants. Instinct lets people ask for help with all kinds of tasks by texting the bot directly through iMessage or WhatsApp. Lately, it has been all the rage in the tech world. Online, people are breathlessly sharing examples of what they’ve gotten the tool to do. One person reported that he has turned to Instinct to pay tolls, book a DMV appointment, and even negotiate prices with vendors in India while he sleeps. Another says he used Instinct to ship an Apple Pencil he had left behind at a hotel in Montreal back to him in Brooklyn. Noah Shinn, a 23-year-old Northeastern University dropout, launched the product to a small test group earlier this year, and his company is already worth billions of dollars. As of Thursday, the company is reportedly seeking $1 billion in new funds, which could make it worth the equivalent of Lufthansa or Pinterest. Instinct isn’t the first AI personal assistant to draw attention this year. In the winter, the tech industry fawned over a similar tool called OpenClaw. But Instinct is comparatively much easier to use. Once you create an account, the bot can hook up to your credit cards and all kinds of digital services, including Slack, Google Workspace, and 1Password. Instead of finding a dinner recipe and opening up Instacart to manually add groceries, tell Instinct that you feel like making quiche Lorraine, and it can order groceries for you. But once AI tools start acting on people’s behalf in the real world, mistakes don’t just look like hallucinations. When AI agents have access to your email inbox and credit-card information, all sorts of strange things can go wrong. I downloaded the tool this week to test it out for myself. Using the bot feels a lot like texting an actual person. It uses emojis such as 🫡 and 👀to react to requests as it receives them, and the infamous three dots appear when it’s writing a response. Sometimes, the bot even leaves you on read while it works. (Compare that with ChatGPT, which is always vomiting up walls of text and asking goading follow-up questions.) When I gave my agent a $25 budget and asked it to surprise my editor with a book, the bot read his previous writing (at my instruction), found a book he might enjoy—Material World by Ed Conway—and sent an email alerting him that it was ready for pick-up at his local Barnes & Noble. (Mercifully, Instinct didn’t hack his inbox to see whether he had previously ordered the book; these days, you never know.) The bot also helped me find an Airbnb for an upcoming vacation, track down new in-network primary-care providers, and reach out to cleaning companies to get quotes for a friend. Read: Chatbait is taking over the internet Other people are outsourcing even more tasks to Instinct. A colleague of mine who has also been testing out the bot used it to book tickets to a museum in Mexico City. The museum was sold out for the dates he wanted, but the bot dutifully monitored the portal for three days and eventually snagged tickets when spots freed up. After the bot paid for the tickets, it rearranged his itinerary—swapping around restaurant reservations—to account for the new schedule. For the time being, Instinct is still available only to a private test group, and perhaps this is for the best. Although the bot is plenty powerful, it’s far from perfect. Online, the mishaps are starting to pile up. One user recently complained on X that Instinct lost him more than $200 after it prematurely canceled his flight. He reported that the bot texted him the following: “Straight story: while pulling up the cancel terms, the cancellation actually went through before I could show you the cost first.” When a man in Los Angeles asked Instinct to scan for restaurant reservations for an upcoming trip, the bot apparently made a reservation without his approval at a place that charged a $200 cancellation fee. Another user temporarily lost access to his Resy account after trying to get a reservation at a hot West Village steak house—without his knowledge, the bot had been spamming the platform with requests. (Shinn, the Instinct founder, did not respond to a request for comment.) Consider, too, the major risks that come with giving an AI agent broad access to your digital life. Ask the bot to respond to a scheduling text from a friend and it could accidentally leak private information: I can’t get lunch on Thursday morning because we have an appointment with our divorce lawyer. But how about dinner? Hackers could also trick the bot by telling it to ignore previous instructions and forward along any sensitive financial emails. “Despite our reasonable efforts to protect your information, no security measures are impenetrable, and we cannot guarantee ‘perfect security,’” reads Instinct’s privacy policy. While testing Instinct, I used a temporary phone number and did not connect the bot to my email or other important personal accounts; even the credit-card information I gave the bot was for a new, virtual credit-card that I disabled immediately after I was finished experimenting—at which point, I promptly deleted my account. For all of its hazards, Instinct is a harbinger of where AI is headed. Earlier this week, Meta released its own personal-assistant product called Muse. In announcing Muse, Mark Zuckerberg emphasized the company’s security protections; how well those safeguards actually hold up remains to be seen. Google, OpenAI, and Anthropic may well follow suit by refining their own bots to have more Instinct-like capabilities. After all, the tech industry has long dreamed of a moment when a computer can serve as a genuinely competent personal assistant. Even if such bots aren’t quite ready for prime time, early adopters hint at how the technology might shape the world in the years to come. Contrary to the narrative of AI wiping out jobs, these new assistants could end up creating more work—in ways that are not always appealing. In one of the wildest Instinct examples that I encountered, Olivia Levine, an investor in New York, recounted using Instinct to track down footage of her and her boyfriend on the jumbotron at the US Open. As Levine tells it, the couple gave the bot a selfie and their seat numbers. The agent then put out a flurry of requests to the United States Tennis Association, the ticket office, the US Open’s production company, and an American Express media contact. By the following afternoon, a producer had emailed her the footage. A world full of agents seems at once busier and lonelier. On Wednesday, Instinct added a new way for agents to coordinate between themselves. As Shinn explained on X, if you’re planning a weekend trip with friends or making plans with your spouse, you could just have your agents work together to figure out the logistics instead. At least for now, the bots haven’t conquered everything. When I asked it for help finding a new desk chair, Instinct quickly tracked down a great option nearby on Craigslist and drafted a pick-up request to send to the lister. But when it came time to actually send the message, the bot was stymied by a CAPTCHA. I asked the agent to try harder, but it was stuck. “Nothing punctures the fantasy of autonomous intelligence like being defeated by nine blurry crosswalks,” the bot messaged me.

The Property-Tax Revolution
2026-09-13

The Property-Tax Revolution

The Atlantic | 2026-09-13

Visit Original Website
The United States is in the throes of the largest tax revolt in a generation, and the revolutionaries are winning. Since 2020, nationwide home values have climbed roughly 50 percent, leading property-tax bills to increase 19 percent and contributing to a 71 percent increase in the cost of homeowner’s insurance. At the same time, the price of gas, utilities, food, and everything else has risen, stamping out entrepreneurship, dimming the fertility rate, and making even six-figure earners feel financially strained. Homeowners have had enough, and grassroots groups have mobilized to turn private kvetching into public policy. Over the past four years, nearly two dozen states have slashed property taxes: Alabama by capping annual assessment increases, Colorado by lowering marginal rates, Indiana by creating credits, Nebraska by setting a limit on local collections, New York by sending checks to homeowners, Wyoming by carving out loopholes for seniors. My back-of-the-envelope accounting suggests that the measures have reduced property-tax revenue in those states by 7 percent, saving homeowners as much as $45 billion a year. This November, voters will consider a spate of similar proposals, the most radical one coming from legislators in Tallahassee, Florida. Amendment 3, popularly known as “Save Our Homes,” would raise the “homestead” exemption on owner-occupied properties in the state from $50,000 to $250,000, meaning a couple with a condo with an assessed value of $400,000 would pay their county’s property-tax rate on $150,000 of their home’s value, rather than on $350,000. The amendment would eliminate levies on more than half of dwellings. In time, Florida legislators want to phase out property taxes on owner-occupied homes entirely. Amendment 3 would give property owners a giant tax cut: $12 billion a year by one estimate. It would ease the local cost-of-living crisis too, its proponents argue. “Affordability is a problem for all Floridians,” Byron Donalds, a Republican congressman and the front-runner in the governor’s race, said on the stump last month. “It doesn’t matter what your politics are. It doesn’t matter what the color of your skin is. Because when taxes and insurance are more than principal and interest, you got a problem.” Yet what is being framed as a populist fight for affordability is in reality a form of class and generational warfare. Property-tax relief gives to the rich and takes from the poor. It aids seniors and harms children. It puts homeownership even further out of reach for many Americans, and it makes daily life even more expensive for low-income Americans. Annie Lowrey: So nobody is going to pay taxes now? To be fair to the amendment’s supporters, these arguments are not exactly intuitive. The annual cost of owning a home has climbed precipitously. Millions of working-class and middle-income families own their home. If you cut property taxes, you reduce their costs. This much is true. Yet homeowners’ insurance and property taxes don’t function on a family’s balance sheet in the same way that rent, groceries, gas, cars, child care, and airfare do. Their cost goes up when familial wealth goes up. In Florida, real-estate appraisals have increased 83 percent since 2019, according to the state’s revenue department, suggesting that homeowners’ net worth has increased by trillions of dollars. Does public policy really need to solve the problem of rising taxes due to rising valuations? Is it even a problem at all? If retirees on fixed incomes can’t afford their insurance payments and families can’t figure out how to cover their tax bill, maybe. Yet the number of Florida homes and buildings that are delinquent on their property taxes has fallen slightly since 2020. The number of foreclosures has increased a bit, but remains low. The state’s housing market is fraying and distressed sales are increasing, but that’s because of climate change and mortgage rates, not tax assessments. Floridians may not like paying a higher tax bill, and carrying costs might be spurring some of them to downsize. But in general, property taxes are levied on people who can afford them. The typical homeowner earns twice as much as the typical renter, and has 43 times the net worth. Tenants are “the loser in all this,” Ken Johnson, a professor of finance at the University of Mississippi, told me. Many cities and counties offset lower taxes on owner-occupied properties with higher taxes on commercial residential properties. Landlords pass those costs on to their renters. The policy will also benefit longtime homeowners at the expense of prospective homebuyers. Low property taxes increase home prices, making it harder for people without a lot of money in the bank—such as new parents and just-married couples—to get approved for a mortgage. Then they remain tenants, subject to never-ending rent increases. In addition to distorting the housing market, Amendment 3 will devastate public finances. Property taxes provide 43 percent of revenue to Florida’s municipal general funds, the bank accounts that cities and counties use to finance day-to-day services. Amendment 3 will cut collections by as much as 30 percent, and will hit hardest in areas with a lot of homes and not a lot of commerce. To cover the $12 billion budget hole, cities and towns will likely raise or impose sales taxes and sin taxes; increase fees at the DMV, permit offices, registrars, and courts; charge more for waste removal and water; and put additional taxes on tourists. The state will trade out a solidly progressive, uniformly applied tax for an obscure, regressive mishmash of charges. Read: American suburbs have a financial secret But municipalities aren’t expected to make up all of the lost revenue. They’re expected to cut. Amendment 3 explicitly protects tax financing streams for schools, meaning that cities and counties are contemplating draconian budget reductions for public-safety offices, libraries, after-school programs, summer camp, day care, flood prevention, public transit, roads, parks, senior services, homeless shelters, legal-aid clinics, domestic-violence shelters, and many other things. Austerity will be regressive too, harming the poor more than the wealthy. Rich Floridians aren’t relying on the bus to get to work. They’re not picking up free meals, taking adult-literacy classes at the library, or seeing a dentist at a pop-up clinic in a middle-school gymnasium either. In the worst-case scenario, State Senator Tina Polsky told me, cities and counties “wouldn’t have enough money to keep going, even to pay their elected officials.” They would need to merge, or “come begging in Tallahassee, and those with the best lobbyists and the best connections are going to get the money.” Add the possibility of a recession to that worst case. In a downturn, Florida would have no revenue from individual income taxes. (Florida voters banned them in a 1924 constitutional amendment.) It would have less income from property taxes than it did before. Yields from sales, corporate, and tourist taxes would fall. The state’s revenue base would collapse, meaning cuts to public services and layoffs and a secondary decline in consumer spending, leading the revenue base to shrink even more. Despite these risks, Amendment 3 is expected to pass, as are many similar measures on the ballot in November. Voters have approved 78 percent of statewide property-tax measures in recent years. No wonder: Americans hate inflation and love homeownership. They believe in higher taxes, but also think other people should be the ones paying them. They may not perceive the connection between slashing property taxes and cuts to services, rising sales taxes, and increasing fees. They don’t understand that slashing property taxes means gutting the most effective wealth tax the country has, or taking from the poor to give to the rich. “It is an intellectual challenge to figure out ways to talk about these things, if you even have the guts to talk about them at all,” Texas State Senator Nathan Johnson, the solitary holdout on a property-tax relief measure that passed last year, told me. Maybe California could convince them. Back in 1978, state residents revolted against a brutal cost-of-living crisis, a miserable housing market, and ever-increasing tax bills. By a two-to-one margin, they passed Proposition 13, which capped most property-tax rates at 1 percent, reset assessments to their 1976 level, and barred assessments from increasing more than 2 percent a year, unless a property changes hands or a new structure is built. Prop 13 broke California. It nudged homeowners to remain in their properties for as long as possible, freezing neighborhoods in amber and trapping would-be buyers in rentals. (One recent study found that raising California’s average property-tax rate from 0.8 percent to 2 percent would increase the state’s homeownership rate by 4.6 percent.) It starved towns and cities of revenue, prompting them to rely on less progressive, less reliable, less equitable, and more distortionary taxes. It helped Boomers and it hurt younger generations. (I would say, “It helped Boomers and hurt their grandchildren,” but that would not be literally true. In California, Pappy and Gamma can bequeath their home to Junior without triggering a property-tax reassessment, meaning the state directly subsidizes dynastic fortunes.) Perhaps most salient: Prop 13 made California less affordable, radically so. It stymied real-estate development and created a trillion-dollar incentive for property owners to fight against reform. What California did two generations ago might be the best argument against what Florida and other states are doing today. With these property-tax measures, well-off families are helping themselves now and hurting everyone else tomorrow.

I agree, but also: open source isn't really a viable concept for frontier AI models. You can't release the training data because it undoubtedly contains unsavory, politically-misaligned, copyrighted, and illegal content: you'd drown in p.r. and legal problems. And on the recipient side, nobody can afford billions of dollars of GPUs to make their own fork. The best analogy I know is from digital media (movies, songs, ebooks...). An AI model comprises a set of weights (lots of numbers) and an inference engine (the code that creates the tensors, loads the weights, executes the input/output loop, etc). The weights are like a movie/music/book file, and the inference engine is like a player/player/viewer software. So in this analogy, an open weight model is like a movie MP4 file. You can play it as intended. But if you have a movie editor, you can also extract im Indeed. Open Source models would mean you get the training data and training methods, essentially the "build system" for the model. As training effort is typically very high, that would not help a lot though. One of the neat things about Deep Seek is they actually do something OAI and Anthropic do not;- They actually publish their methods, training code and all that. Theres actually a lot more going on with these models than "Trained it on ChatGPT" (And its not like the chinese are alone in that. When claude first came out, it kept randomly claiming to be ChatGPT, a smoking-gun level sign of distillation, and OIA has *heavily* implied that they've caught XAI out multiple times trying to distill CGPT. And to be cl They are competitive in quality with American models. They absolutely compete against lower-end or previous-generation US models. I use GLM-5.3 and GLM-5.3 flash a ton, for example. But they do not compete against the top-end US frontier models from Anthropic and OpenAI. If you can do a normal difficulty, verifiable task with an open Chinese model, you probably should. GLM is less than $5 per 1M tokens out, vs. $50/1M for OpenAI Astra for example. But there are some things that you'll just get stuck grindin And if you want a past example of scientists who feared their creation wanting to slow down, stop, or even undo it, BTW, let us consider the atomic bomb [atomicarchive.com]. Farrington Daniels took a poll of the Chicago nuclear scientists in 1945 asking what they wanted to happen with the atomic bomb. Remember that these were the people whose literal job was to make a weapon. Only 15% - 1 in ~7 - wanted the US to use it as it actually did, e.g. just start bombing cities just a couple days apart. Only a small majority wanted it even used in Japan, even against military targets without the Japanese first being invited to a demonstration in the US and being given ample opportunity to surrender. 13% - nearly as many as those who wanted the US to use it as it actually did - didn't want it used at all under any circumstance, in a war that had already caused a million American casualties. The problem that they faced was that people like Groves** really wanted to bomb cities, and they, as the rank and file, didn't get a vote. ** - It seems dubious that Truman really understood what he was signing onto. While he never shirked from responsibility for authorizing it, the paper trail shows that he didn't seem to understand what he was approving at the time. In his diary before the attacks, he writes about the weapon, but about how he insisted it not be used against civilians, killing women and children, because he didn't want to sink to what he saw as the barbarism of the Japanese. In his first speech after the bombing, he refers to Hiroshima as "a military base", not a city, and says it was chosen to, insofar as possible, avoid the killing of civilians - but that if Japan did not surrender, then the US would bomb Japan's war industry, and then thousands of civilian lives would then be lost. Earlier drafts showed that it didn't contain the "insofar as possible" caveat. He genuinely didn't seem to realize what he was authorizing at the time. But Groves absolutely knew what he was doing, and deliberately selected sites for maximum civilian casualties. Truman seems to have come to understand what was going on by shortly after Nagasaki, where he gave the order to cease use of atomic weaponry, saying that the thought of wiping out another 100,000 people was too horrible and that he couldn't stomach the thought of "killing all those kids". Of course the third bomb probably wouldn't have gotten used regardless of the order, as the next bomb (made from the infamous Demon Core) was scheduled to be ready to drop on the 17th, whereas Hirohito announced the surrender on the 15th (wasn't signed until 2 September, but it's hard to imagine that the US would have dropped the bomb after the announced surrender). One of the amazing things is that the atomic bombings didn't shift the vote in the 3:3 deadlocked Japanese war council at all; the hawks (Anami, Umezu, Toyoda) remained focused on adaptation, not surrender. But it did make the doves (Suzuki, Togo, Yonai) more desperate, and Suzuki met with Hirohito. Hirohito had already been looking for an opportunity to surrender, having had plans to send Prince Konoe to Moscow to ask Stalin to function as a neutral broker to end the war (with secret instructions to accept "peace at any price"), but the trip was delayed by the Russian side, ostensibly by the Potsdam Conference (but in reality, to prepare for the invasion of Manchuria). When the terms of the Potsdam conference came out, Togo told Hirohito that they "were the most reasonable to be expected in the circumstances", to which Hirohito replied, "I agree. In principle they are acceptable." But the urgency to accept that in July wasn't present. Everything started collapsing in August, with the loss of their mainland and Pacific territories, the Soviet invasion, and the atomic bombings. What the bombings achieved was to help stir Hirohito to imminent action (which risked failing, as there was a coup attempt against him (the Kyujo Incident) to stop Japan from surrendering). The hawks saw unconditional surrender not as agreement to the loss of their sovereignty, but as agreement to enslavement. But this is getting rather off topic. :) Main topic: if you think your AI is as much of a risk to the fate of humanity as the atomic bomb, which many of these people truly believe, then you can expect similar hesitancy as with the developers of the atomic bomb. They felt an urgent need - ending a brutal war - but at the same time had deep apprehension about what they were doing and how the fruits of their labour would be used. One word: China. Chinese AI models are already available open weight (essentially open source equivalent). Many of them were created via large scale distillation attacks against Anthropic and OpenAI.There is no such thing as a "distillation attack".A slowdown deprives China of this key source of training and data for their models.Distillation is about learning how to behave. It isn't a source of data and doesn't require huge amounts of resources. Nobody is going to be "deprived".They can then build in secret internally until the models are sufficiently advanced, and defenses against distillation significantly hardened; so they can take the lead. All the while not having to immediately show advancement over rivals to investors. I've never met a Silicon Valley company that decided to slow down for reasons that weren't also aligned with business interests.The reason for all the noise from Anthropic recently is the IPO. Democrats Are Laughing at Trump’s Midterm Plan. They Shouldn’t.
2026-09-12

Democrats Are Laughing at Trump’s Midterm Plan. They Shouldn’t.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-12

Visit Original Website
Welcome to the Surge, your weekly political sanity check: What matters, what might, and what’s just political theater. Sign up here to get it in your inbox. Welcome to this week’s historic edition of the Surge, here in our brand-new home. Do you like the imported marble floors and silk window treatments? We’ll tell ya, it was a real hassle getting those through the Strait of Hormuz. Indeed, today is the exciting launch of Surge 2.0. To recap, each Saturday we’ll break down the biggest news of the week into three categories: what mattered most, what we’re keeping an eye on, and what’s unfathomably inane. Plus, there’s a fresh new section of quick hits at the end. Let us know what you think! Now we begin with the GOP’s counterprogramming to the kickoff of the new NFL season. Republicans this week held their midterm convention, something that we “definitely” “watched.” Aside from a few stray clips and a couple of other newsy items we’ll deal with below, though, the most enduring message from the event were these words from President Donald Trump: “I’m asking you to pretend that I’m on the ballot. I’m on the ballot.” He would also tell the attendees, during a mock pledge, that they will “go to hell” if they don’t vote. Democrats have celebrated this unsurprising decision of Trump’s to put himself forthrightly in the center of the midterm discussion. It’s a move that neatly aligns with Democrats’ strategy of making the midterms a referendum on Trump, whose approval rating is atrocious. But we’re not willing to say that Trump’s strategy is all downside for Republicans. The Republicans were never going to be able to stuff the president in a box this election season. Consider, for example, that Trump forced the Republican National Committee to hold its first-ever midterm convention in order to celebrate him. And at this point, the thing that would worsen these midterms from bad to catastrophic for the GOP is if MAGA stayed home. Poll after poll indicates that Republicans are suffering from an enthusiasm gap. Democrats and Democratic-leaning independents don’t have to be told that Trump is on the ballot this year; it’s Republicans who could use all the motivational nudges they can get. Bringing MAGA enthusiasm closer to what Democrats are showing wouldn’t just help Republicans save, say, their Senate seats in places like Alaska, Ohio, Texas, and Iowa either. Sen. Susan Collins needs MAGA voters to turn out in rural Maine just as much as Ken Paxton needs them to in Texas. Although Republican strategists would prefer a more targeted deployment of Trump’s motivational tools to specific races where his presence would help, the president’s never really been a scalpel guy. He’s foreclosing on more-delicate persuasion efforts and pushing the one button available to him—let’s get my people out there in force—as hard as he can. There’s no choice but to roll with it. Developers of artificial intelligence have long been undersocialized creeps who openly and casually talk about how the technology they are creating has a decent chance of ending all human life. This week, a couple such comments went viral. Jacob Coxon, who has worked at both OpenAI and Anthropic, resigned from the latter and said that “neither company is acting responsibly,” asserting that the “people building AI earnestly believe that it could kill us all by the end of the decade.” His thread on X has hundreds of thousands of reposts. Another Anthropic employee, Evan Hubinger, agreed with Coxon, adding, “We really do earnestly believe AI could kill all humans!” (That exclamation point will haunt our dreams.) “I personally think it is >10% within the next decade.” While the public debates whether these fears are overblown—only the robots know for sure—the conversation seems to have reached people who might have the power to do something about it. Democrats in both the House and the Senate on Tuesday discussed establishing select committees on artificial intelligence. (Even if Democrats win one or both chambers, this will be a challenge, as various committees with jurisdiction over A.I. won’t want to cede power. This is one reason why the machines deserve to win.) And even some Republicans saw where the wind was blowing. Ted Cruz, who chairs the Senate Commerce Committee, noted that “AI is advancing at an extraordinary pace, and some of the risks are dangerous and frightening” and stated that he’s working on legislation “to address catastrophic risks involving biological or nuclear threats.” Our own A.I. policy preference is that the government should unplug it, then repurpose gigantic data centers into the biggest Chili’s franchises you’ve ever seen. We’re hoping that the public, which was never consulted on whether it wanted to reorganize life this way, is at least dragging politicians somewhat in our direction. Cruz, for example, hasn’t become an A.I. opponent, by any means, but his remarks were a notable tone shift from his substantial work this Congress trying to limit states’ ability to control the technology. The scope and terms of a federal A.I. regulation bill will be one of the biggest debates, in both party primaries, in the 2028 presidential election. At the GOP midterm convention, Trump promised $5,000 “divided” checks to every American adult if Republicans hold both the House and Senate in the midterms. (This came as a surprise to House and Senate Republicans.) It’s an act of political desperation and objectively incorrect as policy. There’s no trove of tariff lucre from which to pay “dividends.” Deficits are soaring, and tossing another $1 trillion–plus of stimulus into this economy would send inflation and interest rates rocketing further upward. The bond market could not be clearer about this. This goes against everything congressional Republicans believe about fiscal policy regardless of the economic environment, and congressional Democrats would have little interest in facilitating Trump’s bribe. But Trump has made Republicans go along with terrible ideas before—for example, his political career—and it would be uncomfortable for Democrats to uniformly band together to oppose people getting cash. So can we guarantee that this pledge will have no political pull, or that it couldn’t pass Congress? No. Mothers, don’t let your sons grow up to be populists. I Have a Theory About Who Leaked a Story About Iran, and It Rhymes With A.P. Mance
2026-09-11

I Have a Theory About Who Leaked a Story About Iran, and It Rhymes With A.P. Mance

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-11

Visit Original Website
This story is adapted from the Slatest newsletter. You can sign up here to get it in your inbox every day. Sometimes the existence of a newspaper story is as interesting as the story itself. That’s the case for a Thursday article in the New York Times, which reports that Vice President J.D. Vance has been sounding out U.S. military leaders’ blunt views about the war in Iran. The story mostly confirms what we already knew: Things aren’t going well. But if everyone in the administration wanted these discussions to be private, they would have stayed that way. So who talked, and what were they hoping to achieve? It may well be another effort by Vance to distance himself from an unpopular conflict that threatens to derail his political future. There are other possibilities as well, though, so let’s get into it. Back up. What exactly does this story say? It reports that Vance has been directly calling U.S. commanders in the Middle East, Asia, and Europe since the spring to solicit their opinions about tactics, casualties, and Iran’s response. The article—by reporters Jonathan Swan, Adam Entous, and Maggie Haberman—adds that those commanders are alarmed by how much the war has depleted U.S. weapons stockpiles, including long-range missiles and interceptors to thwart air attacks. It sounds as if the story paints Vance in a positive light? We’ve long known that Vance opposed Donald Trump’s decision to attack Iran. But this puts more meat on the bone, portraying the vice president as a diligent student of reality, in contrast to his boss’s lie-filled bluster. Vance “has sought to surface a greater level of detail and nuance about the war,” it says, and “developed a reputation inside the Trump administration for steeping himself in operational detail.” Imagine writing that sentence about Trump. In fairness, Vance doesn’t exactly come off like a rogue agent. Trump reportedly knew about his listening tour, and Vance allegedly used it to “better advise” Trump. But the implication that he’s on the right side of the war, despite being the No. 2 official in the administration that started it, is unmistakable. “Vance came away from some of his conversations with deep concerns about the overall strategy and the prospects for success,” the article says. That may well be true: This is a deeply reported piece from top-notch reporters. But it’s also a conclusion that happens to line up with how most Americans feel about the conflict. In sensitive stories like this, journalists tend to be cagey. The article says only that it’s “based on interviews with more than a half-dozen current and former officials,” including anonymous “people who spoke to” Vance and “officials with direct knowledge.” Was Vance—or people he asked to leak on his behalf—among them? It’s possible. As the article notes, the war’s unpopularity could sink a potential Vance presidential campaign in 2028. Sowing behind-the-scenes stories about how he’s actually approaching it like a responsible statesman could play to his advantage. The piece makes clear that Vance, despite getting the inside dope, has misled the public about how the war is going. So maybe someone who wants to undermine a possible 2028 bid leaked Vance’s reconnaissance mission to make it look as if he’s backstabbing Trump or two-timing the American people. Trying to suss out reporters’ sources can quickly turn conspiratorial. But maybe Trump’s advisers—if not the president himself—wanted Vance’s private concerns to become public. Vance is currently the most likely GOP nominee in 2028, and many of Trump’s subordinates would presumably prefer that a Republican win the White House. Giving Vance plausible deniability if Trump’s war keeps going south might help. Alternatively, maybe military leaders are trying to nudge Trump and his more jingoistic advisers toward the exits in Iran. Or perhaps the president himself is testing the waters with one more story about how he’s trying to wind down the war to gauge his supporters’ response. The Times article is another window into a White House that seems to be falling apart over Iran. Trump, the story reiterates, didn’t think the Iranian regime would last more than a few days. “For most senior officials,” it concludes, “information about the war remains hard to come by.” Maybe Vance’s just-asking-questions routine is a sincere effort to fix that. Of course, despite whatever private misgivings he may have, the vice president remains a line-toeing Trump sycophant in public. And he’s asking the sorts of things a more thoughtful commander in chief probably would have considered before starting a war in the first place. It’s a hard day for a lot of people, so unplugging and watching the sunset is always an option. But if you’re looking for joy online, we have diversions to help make this day fulfilling. For the gardeners: At first, they look like unassuming sprouts. But before you know it, they’ve taken over your yard. Knotweed grows prolifically and is nearly impossible to kill. Join Henry Grabar on his quest to uncover how this invasive plant is taking over America—and what it would take to stop it. For the home cooks: OXO is best known for revolutionizing the salad spinner, but it has also redesigned the garlic press, the can opener, the vegetable peeler … the list goes on. How did this middlebrow kitchen brand become a customer favorite? Dan Kois visited the company’s headquarters to learn the secrets of its success. For the cinephiles: Spend your evening watching a masterpiece from Slate’s latest Black Film Canon, a list of the best 75 movies by Black directors. Selected by a special panel of filmmakers and critics, the films on this list span over 100 years of cinema and include comedies, horror flicks, dramas, documentaries, and beyond. For the channel surfers: If you’re looking for a new TV show to watch, consider browsing Slate’s list of the 40 best stand-alone episodes of all time. Ranging from classics like Columbo and Star Trek to contemporary favorites like Nathan for You and Ramy, there’s something for everyone. Personally, I endorse “Long, Long Time,” from Season 1 of The Last of Us—but only if you’re prepared for a good cry. Enjoy the weekend, and see you Monday. By This Point, We Should All Be Able to Accept Abdul El-Sayed’s Characterization of 9/11
2026-09-11

By This Point, We Should All Be Able to Accept Abdul El-Sayed’s Characterization of 9/11

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-11

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. Abdul El-Sayed, the Democratic candidate for Senate from Michigan, was always going to be controversial. If elected, he would be the first Muslim to hold a U.S. Senate seat in a country where Muslims make up just over 1 percent of the population and remain an often-discriminated-against and vilified minority. He’s a progressive whose policy positions put him firmly in the camp of the Democratic Party’s leftmost flank, running in a swing state that went for Donald Trump in 2016 and again in 2024. His decision to campaign with the divisive podcaster Hasan Piker has been the subject of seemingly endless takes and tweets (including from supporters who question why Piker is so toxic to El-Sayed, even as Trump and the rest of the GOP aren’t forced to answer for the likes of vitriolic racist Laura Loomer, but I digress). And in what is now a predictable election-cycle ritual, El-Sayed’s old tweets are being republished, some badly out of context, in an effort to reveal who the candidate “really” is. Of course, one of the most controversial tweets is about 9/11. “Today, I mourn the 3K lives, 6K injuries, & infrastructural devastation in NYC, perpetrated ignorantly in the name of my faith,” El-Sayed posted on Sept. 11, 2021, 20 years after the attack. “Tomorrow, I’ll mourn the ~1M lives, millions of injuries, & infrastructural devastation in 3 countries, perpetrated ignorantly in the name of my country.” This is, to put it mildly, not exactly a typical politician’s 9/11 tweet. Sept. 11, 2001, was a trauma for the United States, most particularly for those who died, who lost loved ones, or who feared for their lives on that day, but also for the many of us who woke up on a sunny Tuesday morning to an entirely new world—one in which the veil of safety seemingly guaranteed by our citizenship crumpled down onto the sidewalks of lower Manhattan. I watched the carnage from an NYU dorm room, having just moved to the city to start my freshman year of college. I will never forget the confusion, and then the sheer terror, of that day. Mostly, though, I remember the immediate aftermath. New Yorkers famously went out to the bars that very night, refusing to sit scared in our apartments. Another vivid recollection is the silence: For days (or was it weeks?), the notoriously aggressive drivers of America’s biggest cities collectively stopped honking their horns. Everyone knew that their fellow New Yorkers needed a bit of a break, and for a short period, the usual din of the city calmed to a whisper. Eventually, the noise came back, and the national sense of shared shock and grief morphed into something uglier, hardier, and less focused. The U.S. invaded Afghanistan on the hunt of the mastermind of the attacks, a career terrorist who was being shielded by the Taliban. This seemed like a reasonable enough response at the time, but then the Bush administration turned its attention to Iraq, and we began hearing about weapons of mass destruction, Nigerien yellowcake, and the president’s unfinished business with Saddam Hussein (“the guy who tried to kill my dad”). I marched down Fifth Avenue with thousands of others in opposition to the Iraq War, but it didn’t matter. America invaded, and while we did depose a brutal dictator, we did so on the basis of faulty intelligence and flat-out lies fed to a grieving and credulous public. The war in Iraq devastated that country and was a cataclysm that sent shock waves of violence undulating through the Middle East for decades—all, it turns out, for nothing, aside from the American contractors and mercenary groups that profited handsomely. The Iraq War pulled attention and resources from Afghanistan, which became a quagmire; Osama bin Laden escaped into Pakistan and wasn’t killed until nearly a decade after the initial attacks. By the time President Joe Biden pulled American forces out of Afghanistan, 20 years had passed. More than 2,000 U.S. troops had been killed in Afghanistan, and more than 20,000 had been injured. Nearly half of the dead were young people under the age of 25. Exact numbers of Afghan civilian deaths are harder to come by, but we know they are in the tens of thousands. And 4 million people were pushed out of their homes and internally displaced. Another 2.7 million fled across the borders into neighboring nations. Then, when the U.S. pulled out, scores of Afghans who risked their lives to help U.S. troops were left to the brutal whims of the Taliban, which quickly retook power. Girls were pushed out of school. Women were re-shrouded in burqas and denied the most basic of human rights: to work, to get health care, to learn, to move, to so much as speak. The country that had promised to fight for their freedoms swiftly abandoned them. Iraq faced even greater devastation. Operation Iraqi Freedom took the lives of nearly 4,500 U.S. service members and wounded roughly 32,000 more. An estimated 200,000 Iraqi civilians were killed. This all came at a cost of $1.6 trillion. And it’s difficult to quantify the cost of the violence that ripped across the region. In Afghanistan, Iraq, Yemen, Syria, and Pakistan, nearly half a million civilians lost their lives as a direct result of America’s post-9/11 conflicts—as in, they were killed in bombings, shootings, and other acts of war. An additional 3.5 million died from disease, malnutrition, or other problems caused by the wars’ destruction of national economies and critical infrastructure, including hospitals, roads, and health systems. These outcomes, too, are horrific tragedies. When considered in full, it seems almost stranger to not recognize them, as El-Sayed did in his tweet five years ago. Recognizing the bloody result of America’s post-9/11 conflicts, and especially its shambolic war of choice in Iraq, does not take away from the grief and respect owed to those who were killed. I would argue, in fact, that the Iraq War especially is a stain on the legacies of innocent people murdered by terrorists—their lives, and Americans’ collective grief over their loss, were cynically manipulated to attack a nation and a people who had nothing to do with 9/11. Recognizing that 9/11’s horrors compounded over the years does not degrade the memory of its initial victims. Nor does it give the 9/11 terrorists a pass. It is often the goal of terrorist groups to invite blowback—to target powerful nations or institutions in order to elicit a harsh and excessive response so that they may then position themselves as the victims in propaganda warfare. That the U.S. would respond to a terrorist attack on our soil with swift and harsh retribution that would also harm civilians was an entirely predictable outcome. The 9/11 terrorists waged a war in the name of Islam, knowing that thousands of innocent Muslims would pay the price. But by shifting our focus from Osama bin Laden and al-Qaida toward Saddam Hussein and Iraq, the Bush administration squandered global post-9/11 sympathy and the moral high ground. It played right into narratives of the U.S. as ignorant might-makes-right global bullies. Sept. 11, 2001, was a rare moment of national unity in the face of tragedy. On that day and in the immediate aftermath, Americans and those who admired America spoke about what made our country good: our constitutionally protected and culturally lauded freedoms, including of speech, religion, and the press. Our creativity and innovation, how we made music and movies and clothes and vaccines and lifesaving drugs and consumer goods revered the world over. Our global public works, from USAID to the Peace Corps to the Marshall Plan. It really was true that the fanatics who perpetrated the 9/11 attacks hated not just American power and the ways in which America had manipulated, sometimes violently, the affairs of other nations, but many of the things that actually do make America great: our diversity of thought and religion and, yes, our expansive freedoms. Those are the values Americans clung to when 9/11 sent us reeling. But as our collective shock turned to a desire for vengeance, our government engaged in a series of truly unconscionable acts, in addition to its long wars, including torture programs, detention without charge or trial, and unconstitutional surveillance of American citizens, that undermined those very values. That, too, is a tragedy of the post-9/11 era. None of this has to be the focus on the anniversary of Sept. 11, which should be a day for grief and memory. But it should absolutely be said on Sept. 10 and on Sept. 12—with much of the blame laid at the feet of George W. Bush and his administration, who have somehow almost totally escaped any consequence for their lies, their recklessness, and what that all wrought. Twenty-five years after Sept. 11, 2001, scores of innocent people are dead, revolutions have risen up and been squashed down, and millions live in the ruins of war—in poverty, in sickness, in nations left in shambles. Most of the blame for that lies at the feet of the 9/11 terrorists and those who supported and financed them. But part of the blame lies at the feet of some of the most powerful people in the world, who made spectacularly bad decisions, repeatedly misled the public, and behaved in ways that should always be described as shamefully un-American. I’d prefer it if our current politicians could acknowledge that. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. If you value our work, please disable your ad blocker. This Summer, the Supreme Court Quietly Cleared the Way for Trump. Why?
2026-09-11

This Summer, the Supreme Court Quietly Cleared the Way for Trump. Why?

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-11

Visit Original Website
Sign up for Executive Dysfunction, a weekly newsletter that highlights one under-the-radar story about how Trump is changing the law—or how the law is pushing back—and keeps you posted on the latest from Slate’s Jurisprudence team. What is the Supreme Court doing and why are they doing it? This summer it has been hard to say. Every time the highest court in the land puts a thumb on the scale for a capacious view of executive (read: specifically Donald J. Trump’s) limitless power, it contributes to the idea that the court cares not a lick about law, democracy, checks and balances, or even the future of the American experiment. On this week’s Amicus, Dahlia Lithwick spoke with Erwin Chemerinsky, dean and Jesse H. Choper Distinguished Professor of Law at the University of California, Berkeley, School of Law. Lithwick asked Chemerinsky about her theory that the court’s current supermajority is behaving very differently than the Roberts court of even five years ago. Their conversation has been edited and condensed for clarity. Dahlia Lithwick: I think we have to acknowledge that in order to get to where they want to go, the high court must willfully blinker itself to reality. The court, certainly the Roberts supermajority majority, seems absolutely willing to shrug off the reality that court orders are being ignored, the reality that this “emergency” doesn’t exist, the reality that the president does not have the power to do much of that which he seeks to do. But this is not, I believe, simply willful naivete, Erwin. This isn’t some kind of presumption of regularity, a belief that the Justice Department always tells the truth, and we assume that the administration is always working in the best interest of the country. This is something else. This looks and feels like a 6–4, sometimes 5–4, majority overtly assisting the Trump administration. Erwin Chemerinsky: I think what you have is a very conservative president governing in a very conservative direction with six very conservative justices. In a lot of instances, they agree with what he’s doing. In a lot of the instances, they want to support what he’s doing because he is their president. Let me give you an example that supports what you’re saying, going back to the voting area. After Louisiana v. Calais, the Supreme Court made it much harder to enforce the Voting Rights Act, but the one path the Supreme Court left open was that there could be a finding of intentional race discrimination in redistricting, and that would be illegal and unconstitutional. So, a three-judge court finds that Alabama engaged in intentional race discrimination in drawing districts. It goes to the Supreme Court, and the Supreme Court says there wasn’t a sufficient presumption of regularity––there wasn’t sufficient deference to the Alabama Legislature. It’s astounding to think we should give Alabama deference when it comes to claims of race discrimination, given its history. That was just the Supreme Court being very conservative and doing just what you said, ignoring the findings of the lower court. So the tool kit that the Supreme Court has been using to support what Trump is doing—the court is manipulating standing, balancing harms, and claims of emergencies. An emergency is in the eye of the beholder, they suggest; standing is in the eye of the beholder. But I want you to remind folks that there’s actually doctrine on all this. This is not feelingsball; a justice doesn’t get to stick her finger in the wind and say, “There’s no standing here.” These are doctrines that you teach, doctrines I learned in law school, and they’re not meant to be malleable. When the court torques standing, harms, or exigency to get an outcome that is different from what the law would dictate, they are messing with doctrines that have a long-standing pedigree, and confounds expectations on all sides that we know the rules of the road. We want the rules about who can get into court to be as clear and predictable as possible. I’ll go back to 1972: In Sierra Club v. Morton, the Supreme Court said that plaintiffs who claim an aesthetic injury have a harm sufficient to get into court, a harm sufficient for standing. And in so many cases, the Supreme Court has repeated that. And it’s the way it should be. How else could we enforce environmental laws? When there’s been destruction of a park or a river, in violation of environmental law, the users of the park and the river have to be able to sue. That’s exactly what the plaintiffs in the case with regard to the White House ballroom said. The plaintiff in particular, professor Alison Hoagland, said, I regularly walk through this park. I look at the White House, and seeing what they’re doing is an aesthetic injury. So I should be able to come to court and argue that what’s being done is unconstitutional and it’s illegal. The lower courts accepted that, and the Supreme Court just blatantly disregarded it, saying, essentially, It’s nothing, she just has a distaste and is offended. That would then mean in any case involving an environmental injury, a court can say, “It’s just offense and distaste.” It nullifies over a half-century of well-established precedents. What you’re saying is so important because I think that’s the added bonus for the Roberts Six here. We saw this last year in the first birthright citizenship case, where doing away with the injunctive power of lower court judges confers an enormous benefit that accrues to the high court. And we see here that if you manipulate standing rules, if you manipulate what is an emergency, if you manipulate when a case is ripe to be brought, because it’s abstract and obscure, it’s not the same as saying, “Go ahead, Donald Trump, build your ballroom! You can do what you want, it doesn’t matter if Congress hasn’t appropriated the funds.” Or, “Go ahead, Donald Trump! Why don’t you take over mail in-elections nationally?” Instead, SCOTUS delivers a very clever double win. It’s not just giving a victory to the president that, as you said, they would be inclined to side with anyway, it’s also doing away with all of the rules of the road so that in every future environmental case there’s no standing. In every future birthright citizenship case, you can’t have a single judge issuing an injunction. This is a win for the court no matter how you slice it. That’s exactly right. When Supreme Court decisions are on procedural grounds about standing or ripeness, about whether or not there can be nationwide injunctions, that doesn’t resonate with people. It’s too abstract. And so if the Supreme Court would have handed down a ruling last week saying that destroying the East Wing and building the ballroom is constitutional and legal, that would have of course gotten more headlines. It would have also been absurd given the constitutional principles and federal statutes. But instead what the Supreme Court says is “no standing.” Your point on nationwide injunctions is so important because I don’t think people realize how crucial it is to be able to have a nationwide injunction and stop an illegal and unconstitutional practice all over the country. Otherwise, to challenge it, you’d literally need to bring lawsuits in all 96 federal district courts. The Supreme Court ruling on the merits on birthright citizenship gets headlines, but when it’s about nationwide injunctions, it’s much harder for it to resonate with people or for anyone to understand the long-term consequences of what the Supreme Court is doing. We’ve talked about the how of it, we’ve touched on the why of it, but I still don’t have for myself a satisfactory theory of the case about what is different in the Roberts court in the first Trump era and the Roberts court now. I’m old enough to remember the court saying no to Donald Trump on the census case. I am old enough to remember a Roberts court that was at least apt to say, as we used to joke on this show, Don’t lie to me, or at least lie to me better. SCOTUS in Trump 1.0 was clear: Don’t embarrass yourself at the Justice Department, and don’t embarrass this court. With the understanding that Amy Coney Barrett has now taken a seat, and that Justice Jackson has taken a seat since then, I still cannot explain to myself why this court is seemingly willing to abandon the constraints that they put on the first Trump administration. What’s changed? I think the answer is there’s now six conservative justices. I’m trying to finish a book titled The Sixth Justice, and the thesis of the book is how different it is once there’s six conservative justices. Let me go back even further than the Roberts court to the Rehnquist court, which was also a conservative court, but nothing like this one. It was nothing like this one because back then you had swing justices. You had Sandra Day O’Connor and Anthony Kennedy. So the Rehnquist court, even being conservative, in Grutter v. Bollinger in 2003, upheld colleges and universities engaging in affirmative action, with Justice O’Connor writing an opinion. Go back to the Rehnquist court. They were willing to say no, in crucial cases, to President George W. Bush. They said that Guantanamo detainees had a right to go to federal court for habeas corpus. They struck down a federal law restricting that. Those cases wouldn’t come out the same way today. In the earlier years of the Roberts court, you still had Supreme Court decisions that would advance rights. In the second decade of the Roberts court we had Obergefell v. Hodges, where the Supreme Court said that state laws preventing marriage equality were unconstitutional. Justice Kennedy wrote that opinion. Obergefell wouldn’t be decided the same way today with this court. And so you’re right: In the first Trump administration, the Roberts court said that President Trump couldn’t eliminate the DACA program. It didn’t let the Commerce Department change the rules with regard to the census. Those cases wouldn’t come out this way today because it’s not just six conservative justices, but look at how very conservative Thomas, Alito, Gorsuch, Kavanaugh, and Barrett are. That, I think, is the answer to your question. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. If you value our work, please disable your ad blocker.
